Energy momentum tensor correlators in ϕ 4 \phi^{4} theory II: The spin-two sector
Nikos Irges, Leonidas Karageorgos

Abstract
We extend the computation of the C T C_{T} charge of the 2-point function of the Energy-Momentum Tensor to 4-loops. We show that C T C_{T} decomposes into two sectors, the conformal sector, which encodes the value of the central charge at fixed points and an RG-sector that contains logarithmic and constant corrections proportional to the β \beta -function. This latter constitutes the main new result of this work and is inaccessible via CFT methods alone. Furthermore, we demonstrate that C T C_{T} satisfies an eigenvalue-like equation analogous to that of the spin-0 charge, as discussed in part I, though with a different in general eigenvalue. Finally we present three possible applications.



1
Introduction

In part I of this work
[
1
]
we (re)examined several correlation functions of operators in the spin-0 sector
of the four-dimensional
\lambda\phi^{4}
theory. Our main interest lied in the correlators of the trace
\Theta
of the Energy-Momentum Tensor (EMT)
T_{\mu\nu}
,
defined as
\Theta\equiv\eta_{\mu\nu}T^{\mu\nu}
1
1

1

We use the mostly minus metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}=(+,-,-,-)

.
The computations were performed in Dimensional Regularization (DR), in the
\varepsilon
-expansion around four dimensions, with
\varepsilon=4-d
.
This is a computational scheme that is valid near the UV, or Gaussian fixed point where the system can be described by a free
Conformal Field Theory (CFT) but it is thought that it has some validity also near the IR, or interacting, or Wilson-Fisher (WF) fixed point where the system can also be interpreted as a CFT.
This latter fixed point is denoted in the present work by the symbol
{\Large\star}
.
Any fixed point in a quantum theory obeys the equation
\beta_{i}=0
, where
\beta_{i}
are the beta functions of the couplings.
In
\lambda\phi^{4}
theory, the value of the quartic coupling on the Gaussian fixed point is

\lambda=0
while on the WF it is
\lambda^{*}=\frac{16\pi^{2}}{3}\varepsilon+\cdots
.
The fixed point value of
\lambda^{*}
for
\varepsilon=1
reveals the subtle nature of the IR fixed point in the
\varepsilon
-expansion, a three-dimensional, interacting CFT.
For general operators, the abrupt change of dimensionality as the system touches the WF fixed point is perhaps related
to the absence of a smooth conformal limit in perturbation theory and it was one of the reasons
behind the step function-like
\frac{p^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\to p^{2}
(here
p
is an external momentum and
\mu
is a regulating scale)
substitution rule that had to be applied to correlators in
[
1
]
so that they transit from their QFT expression to their corresponding CFT expression.
The technique used in
[
1
]
is the operator insertion method where one constructs the operator as the coincident point limit of the fields and
derivatives that it contains, inserted in a correlator together with another such operator or a number of fundamental fields
\phi
.
The renormalization process resulted in correlators of renormalized fields and operators that were shown to obey appropriate Callan-Symanzik (CS) equations.
The above substitution rule together with its derivative version
\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\to-p\frac{\partial}{\partial p}+d

guarantee the correct transition from the correlators obeying Callan-Symanzik equations to obeying Dilatation Ward identities.
Perhaps the most striking consequence of this is that all correlators that contain
\Theta
must vanish at the fixed point.
This can be guaranteed only if the renormalized trace operator itself is proportional to the couplings’s beta-function:
\Theta\sim\beta_{\lambda}
.
In the next section we summarize the main steps of
[
1
]
that lead to the spin-0 charge
C_{\Theta}

via the correlator
\braket{\Theta\Theta}\sim p^{d}C_{\Theta}
.

In part II we extend the analysis to the spin-2 sector.
An immediate issue is how to define a renormalized EMT.
The classic paper
[
2
]
constructed an improved EMT with finite matrix elements to all orders of
\lambda
.
In addition, through an explicit 4-loop calculation it has been confirmed
that the traceless part of
{T}_{\mu\nu}
has vanishing anomalous dimension
[
3
]
.
However, these authors took into consideration only correlation functions of the form
\braket{T_{\mu\nu}\phi\phi\cdots}
.
Our target is the correlator of the EMT with itself (called
TT
-correlator for short) and in particular the charge
C_{T}
,
hereafter referred to as the "spin-2 charge", that it contains.
This means that it is sufficient to look at the traces
\braket{T_{\mu\nu}T^{\mu\nu}}
and
\eta^{\mu\nu}\eta^{\rho\sigma}\braket{T_{\mu\nu}T_{\rho\sigma}}
,
and construct an appropriate linear combination of them in order to isolate the spin-2 charge.
This approach simplifies the evaluation process, as it avoids the need to manipulate loop integrals with free Lorentz indices.

In the present analysis we use the vertex corresponding to the EMT operator insertion to evaluate the relevant loop diagrams.
This technique is equivalent to the coincident point limit method and offers a more tractable computational strategy.
In this work, by
T_{\mu\nu}
we denote the reducible representation. For its traceless part we
will use the notation
{\cal T}_{\mu\nu}
. The
TT
-correlator can be decomposed as

\braket{T_{\mu\nu}T_{\rho\sigma}}=[C_{T}\Pi^{T}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}+C_{\Theta}\Pi^{\Theta}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}]p^{d}
with the tensor structures
compatible with the conservation of
T_{\mu\nu}
and some other constraints that we will make precise in the next section.

The leading order computation of
C_{T}
in QFT is the 3-loop,
O(\lambda^{2})
calculation of
[
4
]
.
Therefore, to go beyond it, we must compute
C_{T}
to 4-loops.
2
2

2

There are several works on the evaluation of the spin-2 charge, but mainly in the context of a CFT

[
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
or in the context of a QFT
with generalised conformal structure
[
11
]
, though the latter is constrained to two-loop order.

The qualitative change that we expect to see at this order is the appearance, for the first time, of logarithmic corrections depending explicitly on the ratio of
the external momentum over the regulating scale.
The logarithmic corrections, along with associated constant terms originating from the finite part of the 4-loop integrals and the renormalization
of the coupling constant, constitute what we refer to as the "RG-sector" of the spin-2 charge.
These corrections represent the main result of this study.
As we detail in Section
3
, the RG-sector is proportional to the
\beta
-function, identifying it with the source of the breaking of scale invariance.
Furthermore, the fact that the RG-sector is proportional to the
\beta
-function guarantees a smooth CFT limit:

C_{T}
becomes automatically
\mu
-independent when the system reaches a fixed point.

We would also like to give some physical meaning to our calculations. Out of the many possible applications, we will present three,
in an increasing order of non-conventionality. The first is a technical operation which allows one to normalize
C_{\Theta}

to
C_{T}^{\rm free}
, the conformal charge of the free CFT at the Gaussian fixed point, chosen here to equal to 1.
The second application is to the running of
C_{T}
between the UV and IR fixed points, which is affected by the logarithms.
The third is a possible application to holographic cosmology, where the charges
C_{\Theta}
and
C_{T}
play a central role,
governing the scalar and tensor indices
n_{S}
,
n_{T}
and the ratio
r
of tensor to scalar CMB fluctuations.

2
Review of the spin-0 sector and related comments

The massless
\lambda\phi^{4}
theory is determined by the action

S=\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\mathcal{L}=\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\left[\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\phi^{4}\right]\,.

(1)

The Lagrangean in the square brackets
is classicaly scale invariant in
d=4
, which implies that the trace of the EMT, initially defined as the canonical tensor

T^{\rm can}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}\phi)}\partial_{\nu}\phi-\eta_{\mu\nu}{\cal L}
is supposed to vanish in four dimensions.
However, from the above Lagrangean, one obtains the EMT

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}^{\rm can}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi+\eta_{\mu\nu}\frac{\lambda}{4!}\phi^{4}-\eta_{\mu\nu}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial^{\alpha}\phi\,

(2)

that is conserved, but with non-vanishing trace:
\Theta^{\rm can}=(1-\frac{d}{2})(\partial\phi)^{2}+d\frac{\lambda}{4!}\phi^{4}
.
The cure for this is to add an improvement term that respects its conservation and at the same time
makes it traceless.
There are two equivalent ways to do this. The first is to introduce the Belinfante tensor
[
2
]
.
The second is to consider a conformally coupled scalar in a generic curved background spacetime

S_{\rm curved}=\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\sqrt{|g|}\left[\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\phi^{4}+\frac{1}{2}\xi R\phi^{2}\right]

(3)

along with the definition

T_{\mu\nu}=\left.2\frac{\delta S_{\rm curved}}{\sqrt{|g|}\delta g^{\mu\nu}}\right|_{g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}}\,.

(4)

Both of these approaches lead to the same improved EMT

T_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mu\nu}^{\rm can}-\xi\left(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}-\eta_{\mu\nu}\Box\right)\phi^{2}\,,

(5)

with
\xi
the so-called conformal coupling,
\xi=\frac{(d-2)}{4(d-1)}
.
Indeed, for
d=4
, one can check that the above EMT is conserved and traceless:
\eta^{\mu\nu}T_{\mu\nu}\equiv\Theta=0
.
This is a welcome improvement but it comes at a price.
Because of its vanishing in the classical theory, there is no obvious definition of a bare, quantum operator
\Theta_{0}
.
That is to say, there is no classical object to be promoted to a bare operator and then follow the standard renormalization algorithm:
\text{classical}\to\text{bare}\to\text{renormalized}
.
At the same time, the scale symmetry is broken at the quantum level.
In the quantum theory the Langrangean must be renormalized, introducing a renormalization scale
\mu
, where a non-vanishing
\beta
-function triggers the RG-flow.
From this point of view it is rather obvious that the renormalized operator
3
3

3

Typically, the renormalization process proceeds
by promoting the classical couplings and fields to bare quantum quantities, denoted by
adding a label
0
to the quantity. Then after renormalization, the label is dropped, bringing the notation back to its original form.
Since the notation for classical and renormalized quantities is the same, the distinction between the two should be made from the context.
This lightens quite a bit the notation and it is the convention we will use here.

representing the trace of the EMT (the charge associated with the scale transformations)
should be proportional to the
\beta
-function:

\Theta\sim\beta_{\lambda}\,.

(6)

By dimensional analysis, the trace operator has engineering dimension equal to
d
.
Therefore, one way to define a quantum trace operator is to consider a linear combination of all the
(independent) operators of dimension
d
. In the case of the massless
\lambda\phi^{4}
-theory,
exploiting the equations of motion, the number of independent operators can be reduced to two. The initial number of operators with
dimension
d
is four:
(K_{1}=\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi,K_{2}=\Box\phi^{2},K_{3}=\phi\Box\phi,K_{4}=\lambda\mu^{\varepsilon}\phi^{4})
. Utilizing the equations of motion, we get that

\phi\Box\phi=-\frac{\lambda\mu^{\varepsilon}}{6}\phi^{4}
, so there are three remaining operators. These three operators are connected by the derivative identity

\Box\phi^{2}=2\phi\Box\phi+2\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi
.
We call the former the
E
-identity and the latter the
F
-identity.
4
4

4

These identities were shown to hold at the quantum operator level in
[
1
]
.

As a result, the basis of independent operators with mass
dimension
d
consists of two operators. Any two of the
K_{1},\cdots,K_{4}
can be chosen as the independent set.
The trace can be therefore represented for example as
5
5

5

\phi\Box\phi
stands for the
renormalized version of
\phi\Box\phi
operator and not for the product of the renormalized field
\phi

with the operator
\Box\phi
. The same applies to the
\Box\phi^{2}
operator.
:

\Theta=\beta_{\lambda}\left[c_{1}\phi\Box\phi+c_{2}\Box\phi^{2}\right]\,,

(7)

where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are coefficients that may depend on
\lambda
.
The Callan-Symanzik equation satisfied by correlation functions that contain the trace operator determines these coefficients.
Because of the conserved current nature of
T_{\mu\nu}
, the trace operator
\Theta
, as part of the EMT, has an exactly vanishing anomalous dimension, i.e.
\Gamma_{\Theta}=0
.
For example, the Callan-Symanzik equation for the relatively simple correlator
\braket{\Theta\phi\phi}
is

\left[\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}+\beta_{\lambda}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}+2\gamma_{\phi}\right]\braket{\Theta\phi\phi}=0.

(8)

When applying this equation, one should not neglect the mixing effects between the
\phi\Box\phi
and
\Box\phi^{2}
operators.
In this case, the mixing contribution comes from the following loop topology:

Figure 1:
The 3-loop diagram contributing to the mixing between the

\phi\Box\phi
and
\Box\phi^{2}
operators. The black square denotes a
\phi^{4}
insertion.

In the presence of mixing the anomalous dimension is a matrix and
we can write the Callan-Symanzik equation satisfied by the
\Box\phi^{2}
and
\phi\Box\phi
operators as

\left[\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}+\beta_{\lambda}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}+2\gamma_{\phi}\right]\braket{Q_{I}\phi\phi}+\Gamma_{IJ}\braket{Q_{J}\phi\phi}=0\,,\hskip 14.22636ptI,J=1,2

(9)

with

\displaystyle Q=\begin{bmatrix}\Box\phi^{2}\\
\phi\Box\phi\end{bmatrix}\,\,,\,\,\Gamma=\begin{bmatrix}\Gamma_{\phi^{2}}&0\\
\frac{\lambda^{3}}{4(4\pi)^{6}}&\Gamma_{\phi^{4}}-\frac{\beta_{\lambda}}{\lambda}\end{bmatrix}\,.

(10)

The off-diagonal mixing term was first determined by
[
4
]
and confirmed in
[
1
]
.
Combining (
8
) and (
9
) we get a
2\times 2
system of differential equations for the coefficients
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
that can be solved
using the perturbative results for the
\beta
-function and the anomalous dimensions of the operators. We list these in Appendix (
A.2
). The solution is

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle=c\frac{\beta_{\lambda}}{\lambda}

(11)

\displaystyle c_{2}

\displaystyle=c\beta_{\lambda}\left(\frac{\lambda}{32(4\pi)^{4}}+O(\lambda^{2})\right)\,,

(12)

with
c
an overall normalization constant. So the leading order expression for the trace operator becomes
[
1
]
:

\Theta=c\beta_{\lambda}\left[-\frac{1}{6}\phi^{4}+\left(\frac{\lambda}{32(4\pi)^{4}}+O(\lambda^{2})\right)\Box\phi^{2}\right]\,.

(13)

The coefficient of the
\Box\phi^{2}
operator above
receives contributions from its anomalous dimension, the renormalization of
\lambda
and the
off-diagonal mixing term in (
10
).
Of course, knowing that there are only two independent operators of dimension 4,
we could have expressed
\Theta
as a combination of
(\partial\phi)^{2}
and
\phi^{4}
, using the
E
and
F
identities.
This would be enough reason to prohibit the addition of a
\xi\Box\phi^{2}
effective term in the Lagrangean, but
let us elaborate a bit more on this, as it will clarify the connection of our result (
13
) with some related important work.

One could think that if there is a classical operator of the form (
5
) that in the quantum theory
gets renormalized term by term, the Zamolodchikov statement

\Theta=\sum_{i}\beta_{g_{i}}\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial g_{i}}=\sum_{i}\beta_{g_{i}}O_{g_{i}}\,

(14)

would require a
\xi
-term in the Lagrangean, say in the form
\xi\Box\phi^{2}
.
In fact, Lüscher showed
[
12
]
that the conformal coupling
\xi
in (
3
) receives corrections
in the curved space action. The puzzling fact is that even though the
\xi
-term disappears from the Lagrangean in the flat limit,
it survives in
\Theta
as a
\xi\Box\phi^{2}
term even in the flat limit, as shown in
[
4
]
.
In
[
12
]
the beta function of
\xi
was extracted. In
d=4
curved space the result was

\beta_{\xi}=\left(\xi-\frac{1}{6}\right)\Gamma_{\phi^{2}}+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{18(4\pi)^{4}}+\cdots

(15)

with the perturbative trajectory of
\xi
in the vicinity of the Gaussian fixed point given by

\xi=\frac{1}{6}+\frac{\lambda}{36(4\pi)^{2}}+\cdots\,,

(16)

the solution of
\mu\frac{\partial\xi}{\partial\mu}=\beta_{\xi}
with boundary condition
\xi=\xi_{0}=\frac{1}{6}
when
\lambda=0
.
This last result should be another alarm since
\xi
does not depend on a regulating scale, its running being determined by the running
of
\lambda
. This is a consequence of the fact that it is a parameter (rather than a coupling)
associated with an exactly marginal operator, like
\Box\phi^{2}
. Consistently with that,
\Box\phi^{2}
is not an independent operator
in the Lagrangean and its anomalous dimension is determined by the anomalous dimension of
\phi^{2}

[
1
]
.
Ignoring these warnings, one could argue that the correct form of the renormalized trace operator is of the form

\Theta\sim\beta_{\lambda}\phi^{4}+\beta_{\xi}\Box\phi^{2}
, which would clearly contradict the direct computation that lead to (
13
), where
\beta_{\lambda}
is an overall factor.
The contradiction can also be understood by noticing that simultaneous solution to the Wilson-Fisher equations

\beta_{\lambda}=0
and
\beta_{\xi}=0
does not exist. The non-trivial fixed point would be therefore lost, introducing a quantum Weyl anomaly
of the form
\Theta^{*}=\beta_{\xi}^{*}\Box\phi^{2}
.
For all these reasons we will be using the classical
\xi_{0}
just as a useful "improvement parameter"
which is however eventually replaced in the renormalized
\Theta
by the specific
\lambda
-dependent corrections that the
flat space quantum effects impose. As a consequence, the curved space order parameter of spontaneous scale symmetry breaking
\beta_{\xi}
,
will not be relevant in our flat space approach. The short excursion in curved space that yielded (
5
)
is merely a useful trick to obtain an improved EMT.

The leading order 2-point function of
\Theta
is entirely determined by the self 2-point function of the
K_{3}=\phi\Box\phi
operator
because the other operators (only
K_{2}
in this case) contribute higher order corrections. Its value is
[
1
]
:

\braket{\Theta\Theta}=c^{2}p^{4}\beta_{\lambda}^{2}\left[1+\frac{6\lambda}{(4\pi)^{2}}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right]+O(\lambda^{6})\,.\,

(17)

The minus sign in the logarithm comes from the convention on the renormalization condition at
p^{2}=-\mu^{2}
,
which corresponds to spacelike momenta.
We choose the renormalization condition at this value of the momentum in order to avoid on-shell poles in the correlation functions.
The charge
C_{\Theta}
of the leading order correlator

\braket{\Theta\Theta}\equiv C_{\Theta}(d-1)^{2}p^{d}

(18)

obeys the following system of eigenvalue-like equations:

\displaystyle\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}C_{\Theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e_{\Theta}C_{\Theta}

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}C_{\Theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+e_{\Theta}C_{\Theta}

(19)

The two equations sum to the Callan-Symanzik equation.
The solution for (
17
) is

e_{\Theta}=2\Gamma_{\phi^{4}}+O(\lambda^{2})\,.

(20)

In general, the eigenvalue-like quantity (actually a function of
\lambda
)
e_{\Theta}

determines a specific RG flow, as the system (
2
) of differential equations can be solved,
at least in principle.

C_{\Theta}
is fixed up to an overall normalization constant
c
.
As mentioned in the Introduction, this normalization can not be fixed in perturbation theory but it can be related to the normalization
of the spin-2 charge, as we will see later on.

3
The spin-2 sector

This section is divided into two parts. In the first part, we revisit the calculation of the 2-point function of the EMT to order
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
,
originally performed in
[
4
]
, in order to set the stage for the second part, which presents the 4-loop calculation.

The decomposition of the bare 2-point function in momentum space, for a QFT with vanishing 1-point functions,

6
6

6

The one point function
\braket{T_{\mu\nu}}
is given by a scaleless integral, which vanishes in DR.
A clear explanation of the vanishing of scaleless integrals within dimensional regularization is provided in
[
13
]
.

is fixed to the following form:

\braket{T^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}T^{(0)}_{\rho\sigma}}=\left[C_{T}^{(0)}(p)\Pi_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}+C_{\Theta}^{(0)}(p)\pi_{\mu\nu}\pi_{\rho\sigma}\right]p^{d}\,,

(21)

where
C_{T}
and
C_{\Theta}
are the spin-2 and spin-0 charges respectively and

\displaystyle\pi_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{p^{2}}

(22)

\displaystyle\Pi^{T}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\pi_{\mu\rho}\pi_{\nu\sigma}+\pi_{\mu\sigma}\pi_{\nu\rho}-\frac{2}{d-1}\pi_{\mu\nu}\pi_{\rho\sigma}\right)\,.

(23)

The spin-2 charge is associated with the traceless part of the EMT’s 2-point function,
while the spin-0 charge is associated with the trace.
To evaluate the charges it is sufficient to consider the traces of the two-point function (
21
),
according to

\displaystyle C_{\Theta}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta^{\mu\nu}\eta^{\rho\sigma}\braket{T^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}T^{(0)}_{\rho\sigma}}}{(d-1)^{2}}p^{-d}

(24)

\displaystyle C_{T}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(d+1)(d-2)}\left[\braket{T^{(0)\mu\nu}T^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}}-\frac{\eta^{\mu\nu}\eta^{\rho\sigma}\braket{T^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}T^{(0)}_{\rho\sigma}}}{d-1}\right]p^{-d}\,.

(25)

In Feynman diagrams the insertion of the operator
T^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
is realized by the vertex (
A.3
).

7
7

7

Notice that due to the form of (
25
) the trace necessarily contaminates the computation.
A calculation without involving the trace would be possible to do only in the spirit of
[
1
]
, projecting the traceless irrep

{\cal T}_{\mu\nu}
on the basis of dimension-
d
traceless operators and solving the mixing problem.
This has been essentially done in
[
3
]
for the 4-loop calculation of the anomalous dimension of symmetric,
traceless operators in the context of the
\braket{{\cal T}_{\mu\nu}\cdots\phi\phi}
correlator.

In
[
1
]
it was shown that a maximal loop computation could be avoided in the case of
\braket{\Theta\Theta}

because four powers of
\lambda
,
equivalent to 2 orders of perturbation theory could be bypassed by the boundary condition that required the result (
17
) to be proportional to
\beta_{\lambda}^{2}
,
with the boundary condition imposed by the CS equation.
This is no longer possible in the spin-2 sector where
{\cal T}_{\mu\nu}
has no reason to vanish on fixed points.
Thus, the order of the required computation is more straightforward to deduce.
A diagrammatic analysis shows that, as far as
\braket{T_{\mu\nu}T_{\rho\sigma}}
is concerned, for a given power of
\lambda
in perturbation theory
the number of required loops follows the rule

C_{T}\,\,\,\,{\rm from}\,\,\,\,\braket{T^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}T^{(0)}_{\rho\sigma}}\,\,{\rm in}\,\,\eqref{ct}:\hskip 28.45274ptO(\lambda^{n})\to(n+1)\text{-loops}\,.

(26)

In (
2
), we presented the eigenvalue-like equation satisfied by
C_{\Theta}
.
It is natural to ask what is the analogous implication for
C_{T}
.
Due to its vanishing anomalous dimension, EMT satisfies the following CS equation

\left[\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}+\beta_{\lambda}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\right]\braket{T_{\mu\nu}T_{\rho\sigma}}=0\,.

(27)

From the decomposition (
21
), we observe that all the dependence of the correlator on the renormalization scale
\mu
and the coupling constant
\lambda
is encoded in the renormalized charges
C_{\Theta}
and
C_{T}
.
The projectors
\pi_{\mu\nu}
and
\Pi^{T}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
merely fix the Lorentz structure of the correlator and are independent of the dynamics.
Hitting the correlator with the Callan-Symanzik operator we obtain

\displaystyle\left[\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}+\beta_{\lambda}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\right]\left[C_{T}\Pi^{T}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}+C_{\Theta}\pi_{\mu\nu}\pi_{\rho\sigma}\right]=0\Rightarrow\begin{cases}\left(\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}+\beta_{\lambda}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\right)C_{T}=0\\
\left(\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}+\beta_{\lambda}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\right)C_{\Theta}=0\end{cases}

(28)

and it is reasonable to ask whether
C_{T}
satisfies an analogous to (
2
) eigenvalue-like system of equations

\displaystyle\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}C_{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e_{T}C_{T}

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}C_{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{T}C_{T}

(29)

with a different than
e_{\Theta}
, in general, eigenvalue
e_{T}
.
As in the spin-0 sector, the above system of differential equations can be highly non-trivial for general
e_{T}
.
As stated in
[
1
,
14
]
, the proper way to express this is that solutions to
(
2
) and (
3
) define RG flows on the phase diagram that approach the fixed point(s).
Generic trajectories connecting the Gaussian and WF fixed points may deviate from the perturbative flow.

3.1
C_{T}
at 3-loops

Like
C_{\Theta}
, away from fixed points,
C_{T}
is not constant along the RG flow.
In
\lambda\phi^{4}
theory it receives corrections starting at the 3-loop level, with the result
[
4
,
7
]
:

C_{T}=\left[1-\frac{5}{36}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}+O(\varepsilon)\right]+O(\lambda^{3})\,.

(30)

The normalization here is that the charge in the free theory is equal to 1.
We are going to repeat the derivation of this result and there are three reasons for this.
The first is of a technical nature. Among the integrals that enter the calculation, one turns out to
be irreducible, in the sense that it can not be expressed in terms of more elementary integrals.
In
[
4
]
this integral was computed using a representation in terms of Gegenbauer polynomials.
Here we will compute it using the Mellin-Barnes representation. This is not only a more modern, easier to generalize method
but it also serves as an introduction to the master integrals in next to leading order computations that follow.
The second reason is of a qualitative nature. In
[
4
]
the 3-loop calculation was explicitly done for
the conformal value
\xi=\frac{d-2}{4(d-1)}
and it was simply stated that it was repeated for the
value
\xi=0
, leading to the same result. From this the authors concluded that the
O(\lambda^{2})
result is
\xi
-independent.
Here we will perform the calculation for generic value of
\xi
, proving the statement.
To this end, we divide
C_{T}
into two parts, the
\xi
-independent and the
\xi
-dependent parts:

C_{T}=C_{T}^{\rm(i)}+C_{T}^{(\xi)}\,,

(31)

with the corresponding vertices vefined in (
A.3
) and (
A.25
).
We will show, order by order, that the
\xi
-dependent parts cancel.
The third reason concerns the derivation of the
O(\varepsilon)
contributions to (
30
).
These terms are of particular interest, since they ensure the smooth fixed point limit of the correlation function
at the WF fixed point, at the next order.
Before delving into the details of the 3-loop calculation, we briefly present some preliminaries.

The renormalization of
C_{T}
follows the standard procedure used for generic composite operators.
Specifically, we introduce a bare charge of the spin-two sector,
C_{T}^{(0)}
, along with a renormalization factor
Z_{T}
,
which absorbs the divergences arising from loop diagram evaluations:

C_{T}^{(0)}=Z_{T}C_{T}\,.

(32)

To complete the renormalization, an appropriate renormalization condition must be imposed.
This condition should be specified at the same energy scale used for the renormalization of the coupling constant.

C_{T}(p)=1+\sum_{n}\lambda^{n}h_{n,0}\,\,\,\,\text{at }\,p^{2}=-\tilde{\mu}^{2}\,,

(33)

where the renormalization scale
\tilde{\mu}
is a modified scale
\mu
defined for the renormalization of
\lambda
in the Appendix
A.2
.
The
h_{n,0}
are coefficients that arise from the finite part of the loop diagrams.
Their values at the fixed point can be determined via independent CFT methods, see for example
[
7
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
. The QFT result for
C_{T}
includes however an RG-flow sector that is proportional to the
\beta
-function
and consists of powers of the coupling constant accompanied by logarithmic corrections, which are not possible to see in the CFT.

In the computations that follow we will encounter tensorial loop integrals. These integrals can be systematically reduced to scalar loop integrals, where the tensorial structure is expressed in terms of the external momenta and the metric tensor. Several open-source
Mathematica
packages are available to facilitate this reduction. Up to three-loop order, the reduction can be performed manually by employing standard techniques such as integration by parts (IBP) identities
[
18
]
, and the resulting scalar integrals are generally tractable. In the case of the three-loop calculation, the reduction was independently cross-verified using both
FeynCalc

[
19
,
20
,
21
]
and
FIRE6

[
22
]
.

We begin with the
O(1)
contribution. Fig.
2
presents
the topology of the 1-loop diagram contributing to

\braket{{T}_{\mu\nu}{T}_{\rho\sigma}}
.
This topology is proportional to a simple
B
-type integral, presented in Appendix
B
.

\mu\nu
\rho\sigma

Figure 2:
Topology of 1-loop diagrams, contributing at
O(1)
to
\braket{{T^{(0)}}_{\mu\nu}{T^{(0)}}_{\rho\sigma}}
.

The black rhombuses denote insertions of
{T}_{\mu\nu}
. Then the loop integral
that determines the
O(1)
2-point function of the EMT is

\braket{{T}^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}(p){T}^{(0)}_{\rho\sigma}(-p)}_{O(1)}=2\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}V_{\mu\nu}(p,k)V_{\rho\sigma}(p,k)\frac{i^{2}}{k^{2}(p+k)^{2}}\,.

(34)

The evaluation of the
\xi
-independent and
\xi
-dependent parts at this order is straightforward,
with the former being proportional to the 1-loop
B_{0}
integral and the latter reducing to a scaleless integral.
Therefore,

\displaystyle C_{T}^{\rm(i)}|_{O(1)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{p^{-d+4}}{4(d^{2}-1)}B_{0}(p)

(35)

\displaystyle C_{T}^{(\xi)}|_{O(1)}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(36)

Substituting the value of the
B_{0}
integral (see (
B.8
)), we obtain the expression for
C_{T}^{(0)}(p)
,

\left.C_{T}^{(\rm i)}\right|_{O(1)}=-\frac{1}{4(d^{2}-1)}b_{1,1}\,.

(37)

It is important to note that although
\left.C_{T}^{(\rm i)}\right|_{O(1)}
is divergent for

d\to 4
, this divergence is momentum-independent.
As a result, the renormalization of this contribution does not introduce any dependence
on the renormalization scale, thus an anomalous dimension for the operator.
Applying the renormalization condition (
33
) and solving for
Z_{T}
we obtain:

Z_{T}^{(0)}=\left.C_{T}^{(\rm i)}\right|_{O(1)}=-\frac{1}{4(d^{2}-1)}b_{1,1}=\frac{-i}{30(4\pi)^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{-e^{\gamma}}{4\pi}\right)+\frac{23}{15}+O(\varepsilon)\right]\,.

(38)

Using this result we can find the
O(1)
renormalized expression:

\braket{{T}_{\mu\nu}(p){T}_{\rho\sigma}(-p)}=p^{d}\Pi_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,.

(39)

Indeed, the
O(1)
renormalized
\braket{T_{\mu\nu}(p)T_{\rho\sigma}(-p)}
is
\mu
-independent.
As expected, this implies a scale-invariant 2-point function, since the
O(1)
correlation functions
are equivalent to those of the free theory, which is a CFT.

We should emphasize an important distinction concerning the divergences that arise in the evaluation of correlation functions involving composite operators. These divergences fall into two categories.
The first category consists of momentum-independent divergences, such as those encountered in the above example of the

O(1)
EMT correlation function.
The renormalization of such divergences does not introduce any dependence on the renormalization scale
\mu
in the correlation functions.
In contrast, the second category includes momentum-dependent divergences, which may generate terms proportional to
\ln\tilde{\mu}^{2}

in the renormalized expressions.
These logarithmic terms reflect the breaking of scale invariance and consequently, of the full conformal symmetry.
However, not every momentum-dependent divergence necessarily leads to a logarithmic term in the renormalized result.
As we will demonstrate in the following sections, there are instances where non-trivial cancellations eliminate the logarithmic contributions,
resulting in no explicit dependence on the energy scale
\tilde{\mu}
.
Therefore, each case must be carefully examined, as the emergence of logarithmic or finite
\tilde{\mu}
-independent contributions
from momentum-dependent divergences depends sensitively on the structure of the loop diagrams involved.
In the following, we will show that the renormalization factor given in(
38
) is sufficient to renormalize the spin-2 charge up to
O(\lambda^{3})
, as non-trivial cancellations occur, rendering the final result finite.

Proceeding in the same manner for the
O(\lambda)
contribution, which is represented by the Caterpillar topology shown in Figure
3
, we find:

\displaystyle C_{T}^{\rm(i)}|_{O(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=\frac{8-4d}{16(1-d)}p^{4}[B_{0}(p)]^{2}+4B_{\mu\nu}(p)B^{\mu\nu}(p)-2p^{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}B_{\mu}(p)B_{\nu}(p)=0

(40)

\displaystyle C_{T}^{(\xi)}|_{O(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=0

(41)

For the vanishing of the
\xi
-independent part we used the reduction relation (
B.15
) and (
B.16
),
while the
\xi
-dependent part reduces to a scaleless integral.

\mu\nu
\rho\sigma

Figure 3:
The Caterpillar topology

We can actually extend the vanishing of the Caterpillar topologies to all orders:

A short proof of this statement can be given by noticing that

\displaystyle=C_{T}|_{O(\lambda_{0})}\times(-i\lambda_{0})^{n-1}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}r_{1}\cdots r_{n-2}}{(2\pi)^{(n-2)d}}f(r_{1},\cdots,r_{n-2};p^{2})=0\,.

(44)

Let us now proceed with the first non-trivial contribution to the EMT’s 2-point function that comes at
O(\lambda^{2})
.
This is the first order where the diagrams break free from the vanishing Caterpillar topology, at least in the massless theory.
At this order, three different topologies contribute to the correlation function.
In Figure
4
, we present the topologies of those diagrams:

\mu\nu
\rho\sigma

,

\mu\nu
\rho\sigma
,

\mu\nu
\rho\sigma

Figure 4:
Topologies of 3-loop diagrams, contributing at
O(\lambda^{2})
to
\braket{{T}_{\mu\nu}{T}_{\rho\sigma}}
.

The topology in the middle in Fig.
4
is a Caterpillar,
therefore it does not contribute to the 2-point function.
We are left to deal with the other two topologies:

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 82.68 4.57 L 78.11 0 L 82.68 -4.57 L 87.24 0 Z M 82.68 -4.57}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.49496}{0.49496}{-0.49496}{0.49496}{59.75034pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7}{0.0}{0.0}{0.7}{-7.82112pt}{5.14871pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7}{0.0}{0.0}{0.7}{59.92435pt}{5.14871pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}=\sigma_{\rm CE}\lambda_{0}^{2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k\mathrm{d}^{d}l}{(2\pi)^{2d}}\frac{V_{\mu\nu}(-p,k)V_{\rho\sigma}(-p,l)}{k^{2}(k-p)^{2}l^{2}(l-p)^{2}}B_{0}\left(k-l\right)

(46)

where
S_{1}(k)
is the 2-loop Sunset integral defined as:

S_{1}(k)\equiv\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}l\mathrm{d}^{d}q}{(2\pi)^{2d}}\frac{1}{l^{2}q^{2}(l+q-k)^{2}}=b_{1,1}b_{1,2-\frac{d}{2}}(k^{2})^{d-3}\,

(47)

and
\sigma_{\rm SH}
and
\sigma_{\rm CE}
are the corresponding symmetry factors.

\sigma_{\rm SH}=2\,,\,\sigma_{\rm CE}=3\,.

(48)

To extract the spin-2 charge, we consider the traces as indicated in (
25
) and use the appropriately
contracted vertices, see Appendix A.
The two contributions reduce to the loop integrals

\displaystyle C_{T,{\rm SH}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\rm SH}\lambda_{0}^{2}\frac{2}{(d-2)(d-1)}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{S_{1}(k)}{k^{4}(k-p)^{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{T}^{(i)}(-p,k,k)+\xi\mathcal{V}_{T}^{(\xi)}(-p,k,k)\right)p^{-d}

(49)

\displaystyle C_{T,{\rm CE}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\rm CE}\lambda_{0}^{2}\frac{2}{(d-2)(d-1)}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k\mathrm{d}^{d}l}{(2\pi)^{2d}}\frac{B_{0}(k-l)}{k^{2}l^{2}(k-p)^{2}(l-p)^{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{T}^{(i)}(-p,k,l)+\xi\mathcal{V}_{T}^{(\xi)}(-p,k,l)\right)p^{-d}\,,

(50)

where as promised, we have divided each contribution into a
\xi
-independent and a
\xi
-dependent part.
Regarding the
\xi
-dependent terms, as we will show in the following, at this order a non-trivial cancellation takes place.
We follow the same steps as those used in the lower-order diagrams, write

\displaystyle C_{T,\text{sun}}

\displaystyle=C_{T,\text{sun}}^{(i)}+\xi C_{T,\text{sun}}^{(\xi)}

(51)

\displaystyle C_{T,\text{cat}}

\displaystyle=C_{T,\text{cat}}^{(i)}+\xi C_{T,\text{cat}}^{(\xi)}\,

(52)

and examine each term separately.

3.1.1
\xi
-independence of
C_{T}
to
O(\lambda^{2})

We begin with the SH diagram. Expanding the numerator of the integrand and recalling the renormalization factor
Z_{T}
(
38
), we first write

\frac{C_{T,{\rm SH}}^{(\xi)}}{Z_{T}}=-\sigma_{\rm SH}\lambda_{0}^{2}b_{1,2-d/2}\frac{2(d-1)}{(d-2)}\left[-b_{1,4-d}+2b_{1,3-d}\right](p^{2})^{d-4}

(53)

and then exploiting the recursive relation for the
b
-coeffiecient (
B.6
)

b_{1,3-d}=\frac{d-3}{2d-5}b_{1,4-d}\,,

(54)

we obtain the simple form

\displaystyle\frac{C_{T,{\rm SH}}^{(\xi)}}{Z_{T}}=\sigma_{\rm SH}\lambda_{0}^{2}\frac{2(d-1)}{(d-2)}\frac{1}{(2d-5)}b_{1,2-d/2}b_{1,4-d}(p^{2})^{d-4}\,.

(55)

We proceed in the same manner with the CE diagrams’
\xi
-dependent part, which is

C_{T,{\rm CE}}^{(\xi)}=\sigma_{\rm CE}\frac{2\lambda_{0}^{2}p^{-d}}{(d-2)(d+1)}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k\mathrm{d}^{d}l}{(2\pi)^{2d}}\frac{\mathcal{V}^{(\xi)}_{T}(-p,k,l)}{k^{2}l^{2}(k-p)^{2}(l-p)^{2}}B_{0}(k-l)\,.

(56)

Expanding the numerator of the integrand, several scaleless integrals emerge, all of which vanish in DR.
The remaining integrals are primitive
B
-integrals, though some of them involve non-trivial numerators.
For those integrals with numerators different from one, appropriate shifts of the loop momenta must be performed,
followed by the application of the reduction formulae given by (
B.15
) and (
B.16
).
As a result, we arrive at the following expression:

\frac{C_{T,{\rm CE}}^{(\xi)}}{Z_{T}}=-\sigma_{\rm CE}\lambda_{0}^{2}\frac{(d-1)}{(d-2)}\frac{4}{3(2d-5)}b_{1,2-\frac{d}{2}}b_{1,4-d}(p^{2})^{d-4}\,.

(57)

Combining the above result with (
55
), we obtain:

\frac{C_{T,{\rm SH}}^{(\xi)}+C_{T,{\rm CE}}^{(\xi)}}{Z_{T}}=\left(2\sigma_{\rm SH}-\sigma_{\rm CE}\frac{4}{3}\right)b_{1,2-d/2}b_{1,4-d}(p^{2})^{d-4}\,.

(58)

Using the symmetry factors (
48
) a cancellation between the
\xi
-dependent parts takes place:

\frac{C_{T,{\rm SH}}^{(\xi)}+C_{T,{\rm CE}}^{(\xi)}}{Z_{T}}=0\,.

(59)

Thus, we have demonstrated that the
O(\lambda^{2})
contribution to the spin-2 sector charge is independent of the improvement term
in the energy-momentum tensor.
This result is justified to all orders of
\varepsilon
, since the cancellation takes place at the integral level.

3.1.2
The
O(\lambda^{2})
spin-2 charge

The
\xi
-independent part of the SH diagram is given by

\displaystyle C_{T,{\rm SH}}^{(i)}=\sigma_{\rm SH}\lambda_{0}^{2}\frac{2p^{-d}}{(d-2)(d+1)}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{\mathcal{V}^{(i)}_{T}(-p,k,k)}{k^{4}(k-p)^{2}}S_{1}(k)\,.

(60)

Using the form (
A.3
) of
\mathcal{V}^{(i)}_{T}(p,k,l)
, for
l=k
, all the terms multiplied by
(k-l)
vanish automatically.
The remaining loop integrals reduce to simple
B
-integrals. Using the form of
Z_{T}
(
38
), and expressing the bare coupling in terms of the renormalized coupling
8
8

8

The relation between the bare and the renormalized coupling is given by (
A.2
).
It is textbook material to show that
Z_{\phi}=1+O(\lambda^{2})
, form which implies that
\lambda_{0}^{2}=(\frac{Z_{\lambda}}{Z_{\phi}^{2}}\lambda\mu^{\varepsilon})^{2}=(Z_{\lambda}\lambda\mu^{\varepsilon})^{2}+O(\lambda^{4})
, we obtain:

\frac{C_{T,{\rm SH}}^{(i)}}{Z_{T}}=-\sigma_{\rm SH}(Z_{\lambda}\lambda\mu^{\varepsilon})^{2}b_{1,2-\frac{d}{2}}\left[\frac{1}{2}b_{1,5-d}-\frac{3}{2}b_{1,4-d}+\frac{7d-16}{8(d-2)}b_{1,3-d}\right]\left(p^{2}\right)^{d-4}\,.

(61)

In the
\varepsilon
-expansion this is

\frac{C_{T,{\rm SH}}^{(i)}}{Z_{T}}=-\sigma_{\rm SH}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\left\{\frac{1}{12\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{12}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)+\frac{15}{24}+\varepsilon\left[\frac{1}{24}\ln^{2}\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{7}{24}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)+\frac{485}{576}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{288}\right]\right\}+O(\varepsilon^{2},\lambda^{3})

(62)

where the reduced renormalization energy scale is defined in (
A.5
).

Turning to the CE diagram, its
\xi
-independent part is given by

C_{T,{\rm CE}}^{(i)}=\sigma_{\rm CE}\lambda_{0}^{2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k\mathrm{d}^{d}l}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{\mathcal{V}_{T}^{(i)}(-p,k,l)}{k^{2}l^{2}(k-p)^{2}(l-p)^{2}}B_{0}(k-l)(p)^{-d}\,.

(63)

Expanding the numerator, this expression can be expressed as the sum of four terms:

\displaystyle\frac{C_{T,{\rm CE}}^{(i)}}{Z_{T}}

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{\rm CE}(Z_{\lambda}\lambda\mu^{\varepsilon})^{2}\left[\mathcal{C}^{(1)}+\mathcal{C}^{(2)}+\mathcal{C}^{(3)}+\mathcal{C}^{(4)}\right]\,,

(64)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(d-1)}{(d-2)}\left[I\left(-\frac{d}{2}\right)+I\left(1-\frac{d}{2}\right)p^{2}+\frac{(d-2)}{4(d-1)}I\left(2-\frac{d}{2}\right)p^{4}\right]p^{-d}

(65)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{8(d-1)}{(d-2)}b_{1,1-\frac{d}{2}}b_{1,3-d}(p^{2})^{d-4}

(66)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=-3b_{1,2-\frac{d}{2}}b_{1,4-d}(p^{2})^{d-4}

(67)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\frac{7d-16}{4(d-2)}b_{1,2-\frac{d}{2}}b_{1,3-d}(p^{2})^{d-4}\,.

(68)

The
I
-integrals in (
65
) are defined as

\displaystyle I(\alpha)\equiv\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k\mathrm{d}^{d}l}{(2\pi)^{2d}}\frac{1}{k^{2}l^{2}(p+k)^{2}(p+l)^{2}\left[(k-l)^{2}\right]^{\alpha}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{J_{1,1,\alpha}(-p,k)}{k^{2}(k+p)^{2}}\,,

(69)

with
\alpha=-d/2,1-\tfrac{d}{2},2-\tfrac{d}{2}
and with the
J
-integral defined as

J_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3}}(p_{1},p_{2})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{1}{k^{2\nu_{1}}(k-p_{1})^{2\nu_{2}}(k-p_{2})^{2\nu_{3}}}\,.

(70)

The
I(\alpha)
cannot be reduced to more elementary type-
B
integrals, since
J
is a master integral.
Out of the three
I
-integrals in (
65
), it is necessary to compute explicitly only one of them,
since in
[
18
]
it was proven that they satisfy the following recursion relation:

I(\alpha)=-\frac{\alpha+2-\frac{d}{2}}{\alpha+3-d}I(\alpha+1)p^{2}-\frac{3d-4\alpha-10}{\alpha+3-d}b_{1,1+\alpha}b_{1,3+\alpha-\frac{d}{2}}(p^{2})^{d-4-\alpha}\,.

(71)

Applying it to (
65
), the coefficient
\mathcal{C}^{(1)}
gets simplified to

\mathcal{C}^{(1)}=\frac{(d-6)(d-4)}{6(d-2)(3d-8)}I\left(2-\frac{d}{2}\right)p^{4-d}+\frac{2(d-1)}{(d-2)}\left[\frac{10}{3}b_{1,1-\frac{d}{2}}b_{1,3-d}-\frac{1}{3}\frac{5d-14}{4-3d/2}b_{1,2-d/2}b_{1,4-d}\right](p^{2})^{d-4}\,.

(72)

The terms in the square brackets in the above expression, as well as those in (
66
), (
67
), and (
68
),
are primitive
B
-integrals. Their
\varepsilon
-expansion is straightforward using the coefficients provided in (
B.5
).
The evaluation of (
65
) is detailed in Appendix
C
.
In the context of
\varepsilon
-expansion, the CE contribution becomes

\displaystyle\frac{C_{T,{\rm CE}}^{(i)}}{Z_{T}^{(0)}}=-\sigma_{\rm CE}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}

\displaystyle\left\{-\frac{1}{18\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{18}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{10}{27}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\varepsilon\left[-\frac{1}{36}\ln^{2}\left(-\frac{p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)+\frac{4}{27}\ln\left(-\frac{p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{779}{2592}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{432}\right]\right\}+O(\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{2},\lambda^{3})\,.

(73)

Thus, the total
O(\lambda^{2})
contribution is the sum of (
62
) and (
3.1.2
):

C_{T}|_{O(\lambda^{2})}=\frac{C_{T,{\rm CE}}^{(i)}+C_{T,{\rm SH}}^{(i)}}{Z_{T}^{(0)}}=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\left\{\left[\frac{\sigma_{\rm SH}}{12}-\frac{\sigma_{\rm CE}}{18}\right]\left[\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)\right]+\sigma_{\rm SH}\frac{15}{24}-\sigma_{\rm CE}\frac{10}{27}\right\}+O(\varepsilon\lambda^{2},\lambda^{3})

(74)

Recalling the symmetry factors (
48
), the divergent parts and the logarithms get cancelled.
Keeping also the
O(\varepsilon)
terms for later use, we arrive at the following expression:

C_{T}|_{O(\lambda^{2})}=-\frac{5}{36}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}+\frac{5}{36}\frac{\lambda^{2}\varepsilon}{(4\pi)^{4}}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{169}{216}\frac{\lambda^{2}\varepsilon}{(4\pi)^{4}}+O(\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{2},\lambda^{3})

(75)

The
O(\lambda^{3})
terms arise from the renormalization of the coupling constant and will be discussed in the next section.
For
\varepsilon\to 0
we recover the known result (
30
)

Let us comment on the result (
75
).
First of all, it is trivial to check that the three-loop charge trivially satisfies the Callan-Symanzik equation

\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\ln\tilde{\mu}}+\beta_{\lambda}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\right]C_{T}|_{O(\lambda^{2})}=0+O(\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{2},\lambda^{3})

(76)

Moreover, considering the conformal limit,
(\frac{\lambda}{(4\pi)^{2}}=\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+\cdots)
, of (
75
) the
resulting WF central-charge is in complete agreement with the corresponding result presented in the literature

[
7
,
15
,
16
,
17
]

C_{T}^{*}=1-\frac{5}{324}\varepsilon^{2}+O(\varepsilon^{3})\,.

(77)

3.2
C_{T}
at 4-loops

One of the main goals of this work is to extend the
O(\lambda^{2})
result at least by one more loop order.
To motivate this from the purely computational perspective, observe that
this form satisfies trivially the Callan-Symanzik equation
since the running of
C_{T}
is only indirect, coming from the running of
\lambda
. In order to have a direct running one would need
logarithmic corrections of the form
\ln\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}
that arise for the first time at the 4-loop level.

All the occurring topologies
9
9

9

The topologies containing tadpoles are omitted, as they vanish identically in DR.

are presented in Fig.

.

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 41.34 38.58 M 46.85 38.58 C 46.85 41.63 44.38 44.09 41.34 44.09 C 38.29 44.09 35.83 41.63 35.83 38.58 C 35.83 35.54 38.29 33.07 41.34 33.07 C 44.38 33.07 46.85 35.54 46.85 38.58 Z M 41.34 38.58}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}\sim\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{V_{\mu\nu}(p,k)V_{\rho\sigma}(p,k)}{k^{4}(p+k)^{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}l\mathrm{d}^{d}r_{1,\cdots n}}{(2\pi)^{(n+1)d}}\frac{f(r_{1},\cdots,r_{n},l)}{l^{4}}=0

(78)

in general vanish to all orders, as they reduce to scaleless integrals. Throwing away also the Caterpillars, the remaining non-vanishing topologies at order
O(\lambda^{3})
are the following:

(83)

with the symmetry factors given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm CR}

\displaystyle=2\sigma_{\rm TE}=4\sigma_{\rm HR}=\sigma=8

(84)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm BC}

\displaystyle=3

(85)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm SI}

\displaystyle=2

(86)

The reduction of the above four-loop integrals is non-trivial.
To facilitate the process, we used the
FIRE6
package

[
22
]
, which automates the reduction of multi-loop integrals to a linear combination of master integrals and a set of primitive
B
-integrals.
As we will demonstrate in the following, all relevant topologies reduce to the following set of integrals:

\displaystyle G_{1}

\displaystyle=B_{0}(p)\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{S_{1}(k)}{(k-p)^{2}}=(b_{1,1})^{2}b_{1,2-\frac{d}{2}}b_{1,3-d}(p^{2})^{2d-6}

(87)

\displaystyle G_{2}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}[B_{0}(k)]^{2}B_{0}(k-p)=(b_{1,1})^{3}b_{4-d,2-\frac{d}{2}}(p^{2})^{2d-6}

(88)

\displaystyle G_{\rm HR}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k\mathrm{d}^{d}l\mathrm{d}^{d}q\mathrm{d}^{d}r}{(2\pi)^{4d}}\frac{1}{k^{2}l^{2}q^{2}r^{2}(k-p)^{2}(l-p)^{2}(r-k+l)^{2}(q-k+l)^{2}}=(b_{1,1})^{2}I(4-d)

(89)

\displaystyle G_{\rm CR}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k\mathrm{d}^{d}l\mathrm{d}^{d}q\mathrm{d}^{d}r}{(2\pi)^{4d}}\frac{1}{k^{2}l^{2}q^{2}r^{2}(k-p)^{2}(l-p)^{2}(k-r-q)^{2}(r-k+l)^{2}}\,.

(90)

The integrals (
87
) (
88
) are the primitive ones and their
\varepsilon
-expansion is straightforward
while (
89
) and (
90
) are the corresponding 4-loop master integrals.

Evaluating high loop master integrals is a rather demanding subject on its own.
The 4-loop master integrals (
89
) (
90
) have been evaluated in
[
23
]
,
using the "Glue and Cut" trick.
However, the results presented by Baikov and Chetyrkin are not obviously useful for our analysis, as the authors adopt
a regularization scheme different from the one used here.
More specifically, the authors use the so-called
G
-scheme, which corresponds to setting
b_{1,1}=\frac{2i}{(4\pi)^{2}\varepsilon}
in
d=4-\varepsilon
10
10

10

The overall factor of
i
arises from the analytic continuation from Euclidean to Minkowski spacetime,
while the
\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{2}}
comes from the normalization of the loop integral.

, while neglecting the finite terms.
To illustrate the impact of the scheme choice, we present the simple example of the 2-loop Caterpillar integral,
for
p^{2}=1
, that compares the results obtained under the different schemes:

\displaystyle\text{G-scheme: }(4\pi)^{4}(b_{1,1})^{2}=-\frac{4}{\varepsilon^{2}}+O(\varepsilon^{2})

(91)

\displaystyle\text{Our\, scheme: }(4\pi)^{4}(b_{1,1})^{2}=-\frac{4}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{4}{\varepsilon}\left[\ln\left(\frac{-e^{\gamma}}{4\pi}\right)-2\right]+\frac{1}{6}\left[-12\ln^{2}\left(\frac{-e^{\gamma}}{4\pi}\right)+48\ln\left(\frac{-e^{\gamma}}{4\pi}\right)-72+\pi^{2}\right]+O(\varepsilon)

(92)

where the result in the G-scheme is taken from eq. (13) of
[
23
]
.

11
11

11

The analytic continuation to Mikowski spacetime fixes the overall sign.

In our analysis, we are interested in the precise evaluation of the finite parts of the loop integrals.
To this end we evaluate the full structure of the integrals, including all finite contributions,
rather than discarding them as is done in some alternative schemes.
Nevertheless, regarding the master integrals, our loop calculation, presented in Appendix
C
, reproduces the leading-order divergences found in
[
23
]
, as this contribution is the same in these two schemes.
Moreover, we have repeated the full computation of the spin-2 charge in the G-scheme and obtained the same expression.
We will not present this calculation here but comment that the agreement between the two
schemes is not obvious and is a strong indication for the scheme-independence of
C_{T}
.

3.2.1
\xi
-independence of
C_{T}
to
O(\lambda^{3})

At first sight, one may guess that

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.49496}{0.49496}{-0.49496}{0.49496}{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 82.68 4.57 L 78.11 0 L 82.68 -4.57 L 87.24 0 Z M 82.68 -4.57}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.49496}{0.49496}{-0.49496}{0.49496}{59.75034pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7}{0.0}{0.0}{0.7}{-7.82112pt}{5.14871pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7}{0.0}{0.0}{0.7}{59.92435pt}{5.14871pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}\right]B_{0}(p)\,.

(93)

Although this guess is not totally precise, it leads to the suspicion that the sum of these two diagrams
may be
\xi
-independent, as we have seen that the
\xi
-dependent contributions from the lower-order diagrams cancel out.
In the following, we confirm this guess via an explicit loop calculation.

Starting from the SI diagram, its
\xi
-dependent contribution is given by

C_{T,{\rm SI}}^{(\xi)}=i\lambda_{0}^{3}\sigma_{\rm SI}\frac{2p^{-d}}{(d-2)(d+1)}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k\mathrm{d}^{d}l\mathrm{d}^{d}q\mathrm{d}^{d}r}{(2\pi)^{4d}}\frac{\mathcal{V}^{(\xi)}_{T}(-p,k,l)}{k^{4}l^{2}q^{2}r^{2}(k-p)^{2}(l-p)^{2}(r+q-k)^{2}}\,,

(94)

which, after reduction reduces to

C_{T,{\rm SI}}^{(\xi)}=-i\lambda_{0}^{3}\sigma_{\rm SI}\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\frac{1}{2(3-d)}G_{1}\,.

(95)

The BC topology on the other hand is given by

C_{T,{\rm BC}}^{(\xi)}=i\lambda^{3}\sigma_{\rm BC}\frac{2p^{-d}}{(d-2)(d+1)}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k\mathrm{d}^{d}l\mathrm{d}^{d}q\mathrm{d}^{d}r}{(2\pi)^{4d}}\frac{\mathcal{V}_{T}^{(\xi)}(-p,k,l)}{k^{2}l^{2}q^{2}r^{2}(k-p)^{2}(q-p)^{2}(l-p)^{2}(r+q-k)^{2}}\,

(96)

and reduces to

C_{T,{\rm BC}}^{(\xi)}=i\lambda^{3}\sigma_{\rm BC}\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\frac{1}{3(3-d)}G_{1}\,.

(97)

Substituting the symmetry factors, we get the claimed cancellation:

C_{T,{\rm SI}}^{(\xi)}+C_{T,{\rm BC}}^{(\xi)}=0.

(98)

We expect that the
\xi
-dependent parts of the remaining three topologies will cancel each other out.
Indeed, the
\xi
-dependent parts of the remaining diagrams evaluate to

\displaystyle C_{T,{\rm CR}}^{(\xi)}

\displaystyle=i\lambda_{0}^{3}\sigma_{\rm CR}\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\frac{(15-2d)d-26}{d(6d-35)+50}G_{2}

(99)

\displaystyle C_{T,{\rm HR}}^{(\xi)}

\displaystyle=i\lambda_{0}^{3}\sigma_{\rm HR}\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}2\frac{d(2d-13)+22}{d(6d-35)+50}G_{2}

(100)

\displaystyle C_{T,{\rm TE}}^{(\xi)}

\displaystyle=i\lambda_{0}^{3}\sigma_{\rm TE}\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\frac{(d-6)}{3d-10}G_{2}\,.

(101)

Taking into account the symmetry factors (
84
), we arrive at the expected cancellation:

C_{T,{\rm HR}}^{(\xi)}+C_{T,{\rm TE}}^{(\xi)}+C_{T,{\rm CR}}^{(\xi)}=0\,.

(102)

3.2.2
The
O(\lambda^{3})
spin-2 charge

The 4-loop contribution to the spin-2 charge is determined by the
\xi
-independent
parts of the loop diagrams and the corrections arising from the lower-order contribution due to the renormalization of the coupling constant.
The contributions arising from the 4-loop diagrams are:

\displaystyle C_{T,{\rm SI}}^{\rm(i)}

\displaystyle=i\lambda_{0}^{3}\sigma_{\rm SI}\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\frac{(d-2)}{8(d-3)(d-1)}G_{1}

(103)

\displaystyle C_{T,{\rm BC}}^{\rm(i)}

\displaystyle=-i\lambda_{0}^{3}\sigma_{\rm BC}\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\frac{(d-2)}{12(d-3)(d-1)}G_{1}

(104)

\displaystyle C_{T,{\rm CR}}^{\rm(i)}

\displaystyle=i\lambda_{0}^{3}\sigma_{\rm CR}\frac{2p^{-d}}{(d-2)(d+1)}\left[\frac{(d-5)(d-4)(d-2)p^{4}}{8(d-3)(d-1)(2d-5)}G_{\rm CR}+\frac{(d-2)(2d-11)(3d-8)}{8(d-3)^{2}(d-1)}G_{1}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{(d(d(d(2(1309-78d)d-16823)+52181)-78554)+46080)}{16(5-2d)^{2}(d-3)(d-1)(3d-10)}G_{2}\right]

(105)

\displaystyle C_{T,{\rm HR}}^{\rm(i)}

\displaystyle=i\lambda_{0}^{3}\sigma_{\rm HR}\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\left[\frac{(d-5)(d-4)(d-2)p^{4}}{8(d-3)(d-1)(2d-5)}G_{\rm HR}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{\left(d\left(d\left(d\left(-40d^{2}+622d-3737\right)+10883\right)-15398\right)+8480\right)}{8(5-2d)^{2}(d-3)(d-1)(3d-10)}G_{2}\right]

(106)

\displaystyle C_{T,{\rm TE}}^{\rm(i)}

\displaystyle=i\lambda_{0}^{3}\sigma_{\rm TE}\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\left[\frac{(d((178-17d)d-472)+320)}{48(d-4)(d-1)(3d-10)}G_{2}\right]\,.

(107)

A welcome feature of the calculation of these integrals is the fact that the BC and SI terms cancel each other.
It is therefore remarkable that out of the seven topologies initially appearing in Figure
5
,
only three contribute to the determination of the spin-2 charge.
We parametrize the spin-2 charge in the following convenient way:

C_{T}|_{O(\lambda^{3})}=\frac{C_{T,O(\lambda^{3})}^{(0)}}{Z_{T}^{(0)}}=i\sigma\lambda_{0}^{3}\frac{1}{Z_{T}^{0}}\left[\bar{G}_{\rm HR}+\bar{G}_{\rm CR}+\bar{G}_{1}+\bar{G}_{2}\right]+\frac{\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{6}}\bar{G}_{\lambda}\,,

(108)

where the TE integral turns out to be primitive, and

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{\rm HR}

\displaystyle=\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\frac{1}{4}\frac{(d-5)(d-4)(d-2)p^{4}}{8(d-3)(d-1)(2d-5)}G_{\rm HR}

(109)

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{\rm CR}

\displaystyle=\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\frac{(d-5)(d-4)(d-2)p^{4}}{8(d-3)(d-1)(2d-5)}G_{\rm CR}

(110)

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\frac{(d-2)(2d-11)(3d-8)}{8(d-3)^{2}(d-1)}G_{1}

(111)

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2p^{-d}}{4(d-2)(d+1)}\frac{(d-6)(d-2)(d(d(281d-2813)+9340)-10264)}{48(d-4)(d-3)(d-1)(2d-5)(3d-10)}G_{2}\,,

(112)

with
\bar{G}_{\lambda}
arising from the running of the coupling constant.
To obtain its explicit form, we expand the charge of the
previous order to
O(\lambda^{3})
, using the counter-term
Z_{\lambda}
.
In this way, we obtain

C_{T}|_{O(\lambda^{2})}=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\frac{5}{36}+\frac{5}{36}\frac{\lambda^{2}\varepsilon}{(4\pi)^{4}}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{169}{216}\frac{\lambda^{2}\varepsilon}{(4\pi)^{4}}+\frac{\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{6}}\left[-\frac{5}{6\varepsilon}+\frac{5}{6}\ln\left(-\frac{p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{169}{36}\right]+O(\varepsilon^{2},\lambda^{4})\,,

(113)

from which it follows that

\bar{G}_{\lambda}=-\frac{5}{6\varepsilon}+\frac{5}{6}\ln\left(-\frac{p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)\,-\frac{169}{36}\,.

(114)

In the
\varepsilon
-expansion the contributions from the diagrams are

\displaystyle i\sigma\lambda_{0}^{3}\frac{\bar{G}_{\rm HR}}{Z_{T}^{(0)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{6}}\left\{-\frac{1}{36\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[\frac{1}{24}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{11}{108}\right]-\frac{1}{32}\ln^{2}\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)+\frac{11}{72}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{19}{81}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{576}\right\}

(115)

\displaystyle i\sigma\lambda_{0}^{3}\frac{\bar{G}_{\rm CR}}{Z_{T}^{(0)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{6}}\left\{-\frac{1}{18\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[\frac{1}{12}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{25}{108}\right]-\frac{1}{16}\ln^{2}\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)+\frac{25}{72}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{397}{648}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{288}\right\}

(116)

\displaystyle i\sigma\lambda_{0}^{3}\frac{\bar{G}_{1}}{Z_{T}^{(0)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{6}}\left[\frac{1}{6\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{4}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)+\frac{29}{36}\right]

(117)

\displaystyle i\sigma\lambda_{0}^{3}\frac{\bar{G}_{2}}{Z_{T}^{(0)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{6}}\left\{\frac{1}{12\varepsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[\frac{1}{8}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{13}{48}\right]+\frac{3}{32}\ln^{2}\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)-\frac{13}{32}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)+\frac{517}{576}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{192}\right\}\,.

(118)

All the necessary details for the derivation of (
115
) and (
116
) are presented in Appendix
C
.

Summing all the contributions, the
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}
and
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
terms, the overlapping divergences, the
terms proportional to
\pi^{2}
and the double logarithms all get cancelled.
So we obtain:

\displaystyle C_{T,O(\lambda^{3})}=\frac{C_{T,O(\lambda^{3})}^{(0)}}{Z_{T}^{(0)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{6}}\left[-\frac{5}{12}\ln\left(\frac{-p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)+\frac{155}{72}\right]+O(\varepsilon\lambda^{3},\lambda^{4})\,.

(119)

As promised, the renormalization factor
Z_{T}^{(0)}
, determined by the
\mathcal{O}(1)
diagram, was sufficient to
absorb the occurring divergences.
In some sense, this outcome is expected, since the presence of a non-trivial divergence at the interacting level would imply the
emergence of a non-vanishing anomalous dimension,
\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln Z_{T}}{\mathrm{d}\ln\tilde{\mu}}\neq 0
,
which is forbidden by the conservation of the energy-momentum tensor.
Another interesting feature of this result is that the running of the central charge is governed by logarithmic corrections that do not introduce an anomalous dimension to the correlation function.
This is in contrast with the standard QFT picture where logarithmic corrections typically signal the presence of an anomalous dimension.

The total spin-two charge up to order
\lambda^{3}
is given by:

C_{T}=1-\frac{5}{36}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}-\frac{7}{36}\frac{\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{6}}+\frac{169}{216}\frac{\lambda}{(4\pi)^{4}}\left(-\varepsilon\lambda+\frac{3\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\right)-\frac{5}{36}\frac{\lambda}{(4\pi)^{4}}\left(-\varepsilon\lambda+\frac{3\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\right)\ln\left(-\frac{p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)\,,

(120)

which can be written in the more convenient form

C_{T}=1-\frac{5}{36}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{4}}-\frac{7}{36}\frac{\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{6}}+\frac{\lambda\beta_{\lambda}}{(4\pi)^{4}}\frac{5}{36}\left[\frac{169}{30}-\ln\left(-\frac{p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\right)\right]+O(\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{2},\varepsilon\lambda^{3},\lambda^{4})\,.

(121)

This is the main result of this work.

It is easy to show that the above result satisfies the Callan-Symanzik equation:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\mu}}C_{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{18}\frac{\lambda}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left[-\varepsilon\lambda+3\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\right]+O(\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{2},\varepsilon\lambda^{3},\lambda^{4})\simeq\frac{5}{18}\frac{\lambda}{(4\pi)^{4}}\beta_{\lambda}C_{T}+O(\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{2},\varepsilon\lambda^{3},\lambda^{4})

(122)

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}C_{T}

\displaystyle=-\frac{5}{18}\frac{\lambda}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left[-\varepsilon\lambda+3\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(4\pi)^{2}}\right]+O(\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{2},\varepsilon\lambda^{3},\lambda^{4})\simeq-\frac{5}{18}\frac{\lambda}{(4\pi)^{4}}\beta_{\lambda}C_{T}+O(\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{2},\varepsilon\lambda^{3},\lambda^{4})

(123)

from which it follows that the leading order, perturbative eigenvalue introduced in (
3
) is given by

e_{T}=-\frac{5}{18}\frac{\lambda\beta_{\lambda}}{(4\pi)^{4}}++O(\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{2},\varepsilon\lambda^{3},\lambda^{4})\,.

(124)

Let us make some additional remarks on the final result.
We observe that the QFT spin-2 charge naturally separates into two distinct sectors: the conformal sector and the RG-flow sector.
The former encodes the information about the central charge at fixed points, while the latter captures the scale dependence of the correlator.
The presence of the logarithmic correction is a manifestation of the direct running of the charge, whereas the
\beta
-function introduces an indirect dependence through the running of the coupling.

It is now evident that expression (
120
) possesses a smooth CFT limit, as the RG-flow sector vanishes identically when the system reaches the WF fixed point.
Specifically, for
\frac{\lambda}{(4\pi)^{2}}=\frac{\lambda^{*}}{(4\pi)^{2}}=\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+\frac{17}{81}\varepsilon^{2}
, we recover the
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})
central charge of the Wilson–Fisher CFT, as presented in
[
16
,
17
]
,

C_{T}^{*}=1-\frac{5}{324}\varepsilon^{2}-\frac{233}{8748}\varepsilon^{3}+O(\varepsilon^{4})\,.

(125)

4
Applications

4.1
Normalization of the spin-
0
charge

The normalization of the spin-zero charge affects physical quantities.
In
[
1
]
(see (
17
)), the 2-point function of the trace operator was determined up to an overall normalization constant. Therefore, it is important to constrain it as much as possible.
The value of
C_{T}
has been normalized to its value at the Gaussian fixed point,
which was set to 1. Likewise,
C_{\Theta}
can be normalized to this value.

To set the stage, we examine the structure of the energy-momentum tensor in more detail.
We adopt a different perspective from the one presented in
[
1
]
, as we define the EMT in generic
d
-dimensions rather than
d=4
.
This allows us to define the trace of EMT, upon applying the equations of motion, as

\Theta^{(0)}=\frac{d-4}{4}\phi\Box\phi\,.

(126)

The above classical expression can be promoted to a bare operator and subjected to the standard renormalization procedure:
classical
\to
bare
\to
renormalized.
This was not the case in
[
1
]
, since for
d=4
the trace vanishes identically, as a result the first step of the standard renormalization chain was not justified.
This prompted us to construct the trace operator as a linear combination of the operators with mass dimensions equal to four, multiplied by an overall
\beta_{\lambda}^{2}
, to ensure the vanishing of the correlator at the fixed points.
Turning back to the
d
-dimensional formalism, the expression (
126
) renders the trace operator explicitly dependent on the space-time dimensionality, which, in turn, influences the
\varepsilon
-expansion of the contributions to the bare correlation function.
The two-point function of the trace is given by

\displaystyle\braket{\Theta^{(0)}\Theta^{(0)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{16}\braket{(\phi_{0}\Box\phi_{0})(\phi_{0}\Box\phi_{0})}\,.

(127)

The evaluation of the correlation function in the above expression is conducted by applying a perturbative series in the bare coupling constant.
Since we are interested in the renormalized version of (
127
), it is convenient to express the bare correlation function in terms of the renormalized
\lambda
,
which is realized with the use of (
A.2
).
As we will show, the renormalization factor
Z_{\lambda}
renders the two-point function of the trace proportional to
\beta_{\lambda}^{2}
,
where the terms linear in
\varepsilon
arise from the loop calculation.
Apart from the explicit dependence on
d
, another important aspect to consider is the appearance of contact terms. These terms arise from the d’Alabertian of the
\phi\Box\phi
operator.
In the case of the two-point function, the contact terms are one-point functions multiplied by
\delta
-functions.
Fortunately, in the context of the massless
\lambda\phi^{4}
-theory, the one-point functions are
evaluated by scaleless integrals and as such vanish identically.

There are three equivalent ways to extract the
\braket{\Theta\Theta}
correlator.
The first is to evaluate the path integral by introducing a source coupled to the
\phi\Box\phi
operator.
The second is to act with the boxes on the diagrams of the four-point function
\braket{\phi\phi\phi\phi}

and then consider appropriate limits.
The third is to exploit the equation of motion and evaluate the loop diagrams occurring in the
\braket{\phi_{0}^{4}\phi_{0}^{4}}
correlator.
We follow the second route, evaluating
\braket{(\phi_{0}\Box\phi_{0})(\phi_{0}\Box\phi_{0})}
by taking appropriate limits.

As a first step, we act with the derivatives directly on the Feynman diagrams of the 4-point function
\braket{\phi\phi\phi\phi}
.
Then, by using the identity
\Box_{x}\Delta_{x,y}=\delta(x-y)
, where
\Delta_{x,y}=\braket{\phi_{0}(x)\phi_{0}(y)}
, we identify the resulting contact terms. Finally, we take the relevant limit and retain only the contributions that do not involve any
\delta
-functions.
As shown in
[
1
]
, the first non-vanishing contribution to
\braket{(\phi_{0}\Box\phi_{0})(\phi_{0}\Box\phi_{0})}
is of order
\lambda_{0}^{2}

and is given by the Watermelon integral

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{1}\to x}\lim_{y_{1}\to y}\Box_{y_{1}}\Box_{x_{1}}\left[\begin{gathered}\hbox to54.43pt{\vbox to23.59pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.31218pt\lower-4.8955pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

.
Taking into account the symmetry factors

12
12

12

In
[
1
]
, we considered only one of the three possible ways to close the external legs in order to determine the bare correlator. As a result, an overall multiplicative factor of three is missing.

and performing a Fourier transformation, the correlator reduces to:

\displaystyle\braket{(\phi_{0}\Box\phi_{0})(\phi_{0}\Box\phi_{0})}=3\cdot\frac{2}{3}\lambda_{0}^{2}W(p)\,,

(130)

where

W(p)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{S_{1}(k)}{(k+p)^{2}}=b_{1,1}b_{1,2-\frac{d}{2}}b_{1,3-d}(p^{2})^{\frac{3d}{2}-4}\,.

(131)

Figure 6:
The Watermelon (on the left) and the Ninja-turtle (on the right) topologies of
\braket{\phi_{0}^{4}\phi_{0}^{4}}
.

topologies reduce to the Ninja-turtle diagram, on the right in Fig.

:

\lim_{x_{1}\to x}\lim_{y_{1}\to y}\Box_{x_{1}}\Box_{y_{1}}\left[\begin{gathered}\hbox to57.31pt{\vbox to21.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
