(132)

In momentum space, the total bare two-point function of the
\phi_{0}\Box\phi_{0}
operator is therefore given by

\braket{(\phi_{0}\Box\phi_{0})(\phi_{0}\Box\phi_{0})}=3\left[\frac{2}{3}\lambda_{0}^{2}W(p)+\frac{8}{3}i\lambda_{0}^{3}Q(p)\right]\,,

(133)

where
Q(p)
is the loop integral corresponding to the Ninja-turtle topology

Q(p)=(b_{1,1})^{2}b_{1,4-d}b_{1,5-\frac{3d}{2}}(p^{2})^{2d-6}\,.

(134)

An interesting observation is that one could have arrived precisely at (
133
)
by evaluating
\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{36}\braket{\phi_{0}^{4}\phi_{0}^{4}}
,
which is a manifestation of the validity of the equation of motion at the operatorial level.
Thus, the two-point function of the trace, expressed in terms of the renormalized coupling constant is given by:

\displaystyle\braket{\Theta^{(0)}\Theta^{(0)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{16}2\left[(Z_{\lambda})^{2}\lambda^{2}\mu^{2\varepsilon}W(p)+4i(Z_{\lambda}\lambda\mu^{\varepsilon})^{3}Q(p)\right]\,,

(135)

where
Z_{\lambda}
is the renormalization factor of the coupling constant
\lambda
:

Z_{\lambda}=1+\delta_{\lambda}=1-i\frac{3}{2}\lambda\mu^{\varepsilon}B_{0}(p)|_{p^{2}=-\tilde{\mu}^{2}}=1+\frac{3\lambda}{(4\pi)^{2}\varepsilon}+\cdots

(136)

Substituting
Z_{\lambda}
and expanding in powers of
\varepsilon
, the term in the squared brackets in (
135
) becomes

\frac{2}{3}\left[(Z_{\lambda})^{2}\lambda^{2}\mu^{2\varepsilon}W(p)+4i(Z_{\lambda}\lambda\mu^{\varepsilon})^{3}Q(p)\right]=-\frac{2}{3(4\pi)^{6}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\frac{i}{18}\left[\lambda^{2}-6\frac{\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{2}\varepsilon}+O(\varepsilon\lambda^{2},\lambda^{3})\right]\sim-\frac{2}{3(4\pi)^{6}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{3}}\frac{i}{18}\beta_{\lambda}^{2}\,.

(137)

The
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{3}}
term in the above expression combined with the
(d-4)^{2}
leaves an overall
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
term.
Expanding the full bare 2-point function we arrive at the following expression:

\braket{\Theta^{(0)}\Theta^{(0)}}=-\frac{i}{144(4\pi)^{6}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[\varepsilon^{3}\lambda^{2}-6\varepsilon\frac{\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{2}}+O(\varepsilon^{3}\lambda^{2},\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{3})\right]\sim-\frac{i}{144(4\pi)^{6}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[\beta_{\lambda}^{2}++O(\varepsilon^{3}\lambda^{2},\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{3})\right]p^{4}\,.

(138)

As shown in the previous section, the total EMT is (re)normalized by a
Z_{T}^{(0)}
which is determined by the
O(1)
diagrams of the
\braket{T_{\mu\nu}T_{\rho\sigma}}
:

Z_{T}^{(0)}=-\frac{b_{1,1}}{4(d^{2}-1)}\,.

(139)

Adapting this normalization we conclude that

\braket{\Theta\Theta}=\frac{5}{24}\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{4}}\left[\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{2}-6\varepsilon\frac{\lambda^{3}}{(4\pi)^{2}}+O(\varepsilon^{3}\lambda^{2},\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{3})\right]p^{4}\,

(140)

or more conveniently

\braket{\Theta\Theta}\equiv\frac{1}{Z_{T}^{(0)}}\braket{\Theta^{(0)}\Theta^{(0)}}=\frac{5}{24(4\pi)^{4}}\beta_{\lambda}^{2}p^{4}+O(\lambda^{4})\,.

(141)

Compairing this with (
17
), the normalization constant
c
gets fixed to

c^{2}=\frac{5}{24(4\pi)^{4}}\,.

(142)

Taking the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
of the renormalized expression we get

\braket{\Theta\Theta}_{\varepsilon\to 0}=0+O(\lambda^{4})\,,

(143)

where the
O(\lambda^{4})
contribution comes from the purely 4-dimensional part of
\beta_{\lambda}^{2}
.
This result is consistent with the one presented in
[
1
]
.
The advantage of working with a trace in
d
-dimensions is that the
\beta_{\lambda}^{2}
term emerges naturally from an explicit loop calculation. However, the drawback is that reproducing the logarithmic corrections in
[
1
]
requires a higher loop renormalization.

4.2
The RG flow of
C_{T}

A monotonicity theorem for the RG flow in generic renormalizable field theories is well established in two dimensions, known as the
c
-theorem
[
24
]
, where the
c
-function decreases monotonically and becomes stationary at the fixed points.
This theorem is constructed from the correlation functions of the EMT, exploiting the relatively simple tensorial structure in two dimensions.
In dimensions
d>2
, an analogous proof of a
c
-theorem is lacking. The primary obstacle is the increased number of independent tensor structures in the two-point function
\langle T_{\mu\nu}T_{\rho\sigma}\rangle

[
25
]
. For
d=4
, a monotonicity theorem is instead provided by the
a
-theorem
[
26
]
, which adopts a distinct perspective from the two-dimensional case, focusing on the trace anomaly coefficient
a
rather than the spin-2 charge
C_{T}
.

In
[
4
]
, it was stated that the
O(\lambda^{2})
spin-two charge
C_{T}
is incompatible with a
c
-function,
since it is not stationary at the WF fixed point.
Let us re-evaluate this conclusion in light of the 4-loop result (
121
).
We define the parameter
x
as

x\equiv-\frac{p^{2}}{\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\,,

(144)

in terms of which the extremal values of
C_{T}
are at:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial C_{T}}{\partial\lambda}=0\Rightarrow\begin{cases}\lambda_{\rm max}&=0\,,\quad\text{global maximum}\\
\lambda_{\rm min}&=\frac{28\varepsilon+60}{1395-270\ln(x)}+\frac{2\varepsilon}{9}\,,\quad\text{global minimum}\end{cases}

(145)

Clearly whether
\lambda_{\rm min}
coincides with
\lambda^{*}
depends on the value of
x
.
In particular
\lambda_{\min}=\lambda^{*}
for
x=e^{\frac{128}{85\varepsilon+45}-\frac{2}{\varepsilon}+\frac{31}{6}}|_{\varepsilon=1}\equiv x^{*}
.
We can construct RG flow curves, for example by keeping
x
fixed, which is a particular way to flow, among others.

\star
\bullet
\lambda
C_{T}
\lambda_{\min}>\lambda^{*}
\lambda_{\min}=\lambda^{*}
\lambda_{\min}<\lambda^{*}

Figure 7:
Qualitive plot of the running of
C_{T}
for
d=4-\varepsilon
. Each curve in this plot is generated by keeping the parameter
x
fixed. The dotted curve corresponds to
x>x^{*}
, the solid curve to
x=x^{*}
and the dashed curve to
x<x^{*}
.

We then distinguish three kinematic regimes:

•

For
x=x^{*}
,
C_{T}
is monotonic throughout the RG flow and is also stationary at the Wilson–Fisher fixed point.

•

For
x<x^{*}
, the charge satisfies a weak version of the
c
-theorem, namely
C_{T}^{\rm(UV)}>C_{T}^{\rm(IR)}
,
but it is neither monotonic throughout the RG flow nor stationary at the fixed points.

•

For
x>x^{*}
, the charge still satisfies a weak version of the
c
-theorem and is monotonic along the flow; however,
it is not stationary at the fixed points.

Even though the 3-loop result of
[
4
]
does not have a kinematic dependence, its
RG flow falls in the third category, the dotted curve in Fig.
7
.

An alternative way to draw the RG flow is to keep instead of
x
, the external momentum fixed, in which case
C_{T}

becomes a function of
\tilde{\mu}
. We have checked numerically that the three types of flows in
Fig.
7
persist.

4.3
Holographic cosmology

Another application comes from holographic cosmology.
Before discussing the specifics of the application,
we will outline the key cosmological observables against which we will test our results.
Two important observed quantities are the power spectra of the scalar and tensor fluctuations of the CMB.
Adapting standard cosmological parametrization, these power spectra are given by:

\displaystyle\Delta_{S}^{2}(p)

\displaystyle=\left.\Delta_{S}^{2}\right|_{p^{2}=-\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\left(\frac{p}{\tilde{\mu}}\right)^{n_{s}(p)-1}

(146)

\displaystyle\Delta_{T}^{2}(p)

\displaystyle=\left.\Delta_{T}^{2}\right|_{p^{2}=-\tilde{\mu}^{2}}\left(\frac{p}{\tilde{\mu}}\right)^{n_{T}(p)}\,,

(147)

where
p
is the observed wave number (or equivalently frequency) and
\tilde{\mu}
is a chosen pivot frequency.
As suggested by the chosen notation, the pivot frequency will be identified with the DR renormalization energy scale, and
the wave number with the external momentum of the correlator.
The two quantities
n_{S}
and
n_{T}
that characterize the power spectra are the
scalar and tensor CMB fluctuation spectral indices.

The scalar spectral index
n_{S}
has been directly measured and given by
[
27
]
:

n_{S}^{({\rm obs})}\simeq 0.964\,.

(148)

The tensor spectral index is not directly measured yet, but its value is constrained

[
28
]
:

-1.37\lesssim n_{T}^{({\rm obs})}\lesssim 0.42\,.

(149)

A negative valued
n_{T}
typically indicates a slow-roll type of inflationary scenario, while a non-negative value possibly some kind of alternative inflationary scenario.
A third observable is the ratio of the tensor over the scalar fluctuations, with an upper bound constraint
[
27
]
:

r=\frac{\Delta_{T}^{2}(p)}{\Delta_{S}^{2}(p)}\,,\hskip 14.22636ptr^{(\rm obs)}\lesssim 0.036\,.

(150)

Focusing from now on the holographic point of view, let us suppose that the dual QFT to the inflationary epoch is a real scalar with a
\lambda\phi^{4}
interaction.
The holographic system by its nature then requires the coefficients
C_{T}
and
C_{\Theta}
to be evaluated near the WF fixed point.
This is also supported by the almost scale-invariant CMB, observed by the experiments.

The "distance"
d_{\lambda}=\lambda-\lambda^{*}
from the fixed point where the physical system is supposed to be sitting is,
in principle, a free parameter.
It can be however fixed from a precisely measured quantity, such as the scalar index
n_{S}
.
Then a number of other cosmological parameters will be predicted, given a pair of chosen eigenvalues
e_{\Theta}
and
e_{T}
.
Alternatively, there could be dual information about this distance coming from the bulk, where the small breaking of scale invariance
may have a thermal origin
[
29
]
. For a bulk with a scalar in a dS background, it will be related to the intrinsic temperature, proportional to the dS curvature.
We will return to these questions in more detail in the near future.
Here we will just present some simple consequences of this idea, some of which have already appeared in
[
14
]
.

The basis for the holographic interpretation of cosmological parameters was proposed by
[
30
]
.
The connection of these parameters with the anomalous dimension of composite operators in the dual QFT (a scalar
\phi^{4}
theory like here) was made in
[
31
]
.
More details of this connection for a general QFT were given in
[
32
,
33
]
, whose analysis can be summarized by the following relation
between the CMB scalar and tensor power spectra and the QFT charges
C_{\Theta}

and
C_{T}
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{S}^{2}(p)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{C_{\Theta}(p)}

(151)

\displaystyle\Delta_{T}^{2}(p)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{C_{T}(p)}\,.

(152)

We will therefore expand below the three cosmological observables around the fixed point.
Before doing so, we can make one general comment.
From the definition (
150
) and the holographic formulae (
151
) and (
152
) the ratio
r
can be written as

\displaystyle r=\frac{1}{8}\frac{C_{\Theta}(p)}{C_{T}(p)}\,,

(153)

from which we can infer that since
C_{\Theta}\sim\beta_{\lambda}^{2}
and
C_{T}=1+\cdots
, the ratio will be small enough to be compatible with observations sufficiently near the interacting fixed point
where
\beta_{\lambda}\to 0
, for generic values of the charges.
We can now expand the above expression for small values of the deviation
d_{\lambda}
from the fixed point:

\displaystyle r=\frac{1}{8}\left\{\left.\frac{C_{\Theta}}{C_{T}}\right|_{\lambda=\lambda^{*}}+d_{\lambda}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\frac{C_{\Theta}}{C_{T}}\right]_{\lambda=\lambda^{*}}+\frac{d_{\lambda}^{2}}{2}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\lambda^{2}}\frac{C_{\Theta}}{C_{T}}\right]_{\lambda=\lambda^{*}}+\cdots\right\}

(154)

The first term is zero at the fixed point.
The linear term in
d_{\lambda}
also vanishes since the derivate of the ratio is zero at the fixed point.
Thus, the parameter
r
is determined by the second derivative.
Using (
2
) and (
3
), (
154
) reduces to

r=\frac{1}{8}\frac{d_{\lambda}^{2}}{2}\left\{\frac{C_{\Theta}}{C_{T}}\left[\frac{e_{\Theta}}{\beta_{\lambda}^{2}}-\frac{e_{\Theta}^{\prime}\beta_{\lambda}^{\prime}}{\beta_{\lambda}^{2}}\right]\right\}_{\lambda=\lambda^{*}}+O(d_{\lambda}^{3})

(155)

with the prime denoting a
\lambda
-derivative.
Recalling that

C_{\Theta}=\frac{\braket{\Theta\Theta}}{(d-1)^{2}}=\frac{5}{24(4\pi)^{4}}\frac{\beta_{\lambda}^{2}}{(d-1)^{2}}+O(\varepsilon^{3}\lambda^{2},\varepsilon^{2}\lambda^{3},\lambda^{4})\,,

(156)

this becomes

r=\frac{1}{8}\frac{d_{\lambda}^{2}}{2}\frac{5}{24(4\pi)^{4}(3-\varepsilon)^{2}}\frac{1}{C_{T}^{*}}\left[e_{\Theta}^{*}-(e_{\Theta}^{\prime})^{*}(\beta_{\lambda}^{\prime})^{*}\right]\,.

(157)

Expanding in powers of
\varepsilon
and then setting
\varepsilon\to 1
we obtain:

r\simeq 8\times 10^{-9}d_{\lambda}^{2}\,,

(158)

which is easily compatible with current observational constraints.

The situation is less clear for the other two indices.
One can see that it is actually the eigenvalues
e_{\Theta}
and
e_{T}
that determine them:

\displaystyle n_{S}

\displaystyle=1-e_{\Theta}=1-\left(e_{\Theta}|_{\lambda^{*}}+d_{\lambda}\left.\frac{\partial e_{\Theta}}{\partial\lambda}\right|_{\lambda^{*}}+\cdots\right)

(159)

\displaystyle n_{T}

\displaystyle=-e_{T}=-\left(e_{T}|_{\lambda^{*}}+d_{\lambda}\left.\frac{\partial e_{T}}{\partial\lambda}\right|_{\lambda^{*}}+\cdots\right)\,.

(160)

Let us test our perturbative formulae on these expressions as well.
From the leading order expression of
e_{\Theta}
(
20
) we obtain:

n_{S}=1-2\left(\Gamma_{\phi^{4}}^{*}-\left.\frac{\partial\Gamma_{\phi^{4}}}{\partial\lambda}\right|_{\lambda^{*}}d_{\lambda}+\cdots\right)

(161)

It is easy to see that this can not lead to acceptable values, meaning that
the perturbative
e_{\Theta}
does not reproduce the cosmological data for
n_{S}
.

Regarding the spectral index of the tensor fluctuations,
since
e_{T}\sim\beta_{\lambda}
the leading order contribution turns out to be linear in
d_{\lambda}
:

n_{T}=\frac{5}{18(4\pi)^{4}}\lambda^{*}\left.\frac{\partial\beta_{\lambda}}{\partial\lambda}\right|_{\lambda^{*}}d_{\lambda}+O(d_{\lambda}^{2})\simeq 5\times 10^{-3}d_{\lambda}\,,

(162)

which lies within the allowed values, while its negative sign most likely has implications
for the nature of the dual inflationary scenario.

Some comments on the formulae (
157
), (
161
) and (
162
).
All three observables are essentially determined by the eigenvalues
e_{\Theta}
and
e_{T}

(with the latter proportional to the
\beta_{\lambda}
).
The
\beta
-function for
d=4-\varepsilon
has positive slope in the vicinity of the WF fixed point (see Fig.
8
), so
n_{T}
is negative. As metioned the pertubative
e_{\Theta}
fails to reproduce the observed value for
n_{S}
.
A possible way around this is to recall an earlier statement we made, that general values of
e_{\Theta}
and
e_{T}

define through equations (
2
) and (
3
) general RG-flows towards the WF fixed point.
By general we mean here "not necessarily perturbative". For example in
[
14
]
it is proposed that the RG-flow characterized by

e_{\Theta}=2\gamma_{\phi}
explains well the value of
n_{S}
, near the WF point, where it becomes the critical exponent
\eta\simeq 0.036
.
Supposing an RG flow characterized by
e_{\theta}=\eta\simeq 0.036
and
(e_{\Theta}^{\prime})^{*}\simeq 0
, we get

\displaystyle n_{S}

\displaystyle\simeq 1-0.036=0.964

(163)

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle\simeq 4\times 10^{-9}

(164)

while
n_{T}
retains its value given by (
162
), as it is determined solely by the
\beta
-function.

5
Conclusions

From a 4-loop calculation, we extracted the
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3})
contribution to the spin-2 charge,
C_{T}
, and demonstrated that it decomposes into two sectors: a conformal and an RG-sector.
The latter is proportional to the
\beta
-function therefore cannot be accessed via purely CFT methods.
As such, a QFT framework is needed to extract information about this RG-sector.
Furthermore, we verified that the spin-2 charge satisfies an eigenvalue equation analogous to that of the spin-0 charge, with a distinct eigenvalue
e_{T}
proportional to the
\beta
-function. Also,
C_{T}
exhibits a smooth CFT limit:
it becomes regulating scale independent as the system reaches a fixed point, thereby restoring scale invariance.
In addition, we showed that the
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3})
corrections introduce an explicit dependence of
C_{T}
on the external momenta, affecting its behavior along the RG flow.
As a possible physical application, we applied our results to the holographic cosmology senario and illustrated that the three main
observables (
n_{S}
,
r
, and
n_{T}
) can be expressed in terms of the eigenvalues
e_{\Theta}
and
e_{T}
.
While the perturbative value of
e_{\Theta}
does not reproduce the observed value pf
n_{S}
, we showed that an alternative choice for this eigenvalue brings all three observables within the allowed window from observations.
A self-contained analysis in the bulk is required to determine whether this choice can be justified.
